\section{Unveiling YGS-Packer: A Novel Android Spyware Family Leveraging
iApp Frameworks and Native ClassLoader
Hijacking}\label{unveiling-ygs-packer-a-novel-android-spyware-family-leveraging-iapp-frameworks-and-native-classloader-hijacking}

\section{Abstract}\label{abstract}

This report details the discovery of the YGS-2026 malware family, a
sophisticated Android information stealer first identified in early
2026. The malware employs a unique multi-layer obfuscation strategy,
including Unreal Engine 4 asset inflation to bypass scanners and a
custom native-layer decryption engine ("slky" algorithm). Notably, it
weaponizes the iApp rapid development framework to execute dynamic
Lua-based payloads, enabling real-time surveillance capabilities such as
silent camera capture, keylogging via Accessibility Services, and direct
MySQL data exfiltration. The findings reveal a bridge between amateur
development tools and professional-grade anti-forensic techniques. This
report mainly contains \textbf{Forensic Intelligence Report:
{[}YGS-2026-01{]}} and \textbf{Forensic Intelligence Report:
{[}YGS-2026-02{]}}.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Forensic Intelligence Report:
{[}YGS-2026-01{]}}}{Forensic Intelligence Report: {[}YGS-2026-01{]}}}\label{forensic-intelligence-report-ygs-2026-01}

\textbf{Subject:} Analysis of Obfuscated "PUBG Tool" Spyware

\textbf{Threat Category:} Trojanized Utility / Information Stealer

\textbf{Target Platform:} Android (ARMv8/ARMv7/x86)

\textbf{Security Level:} Confidential / Forensic Investigation

\textbf{Author:} Apich Organization Security Team

\textbf{DOI:} 10.5281/zenodo.18320725

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{1. Executive
Summary}}{1. Executive Summary}}\label{1-executive-summary}

A detailed forensic investigation has been conducted on a malicious
Android application masquerading as a game optimization utility ("PUBG
Tool"). The application utilizes a \textbf{multi-stage, native-backed
execution chain} to deploy a covert spying payload. The malware
leverages social engineering (UE4 decoy assets), native-layer
decryption, and runtime ClassLoader hijacking to bypass traditional
static analysis and Play Protect heuristics.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{2. Threat Infrastructure \& Social
Engineering}}{2. Threat Infrastructure \& Social Engineering}}\label{2-threat-infrastructure--social-engineering}

The attacker employs several layers of deception to maintain a low
profile on the victim\textquotesingle s device:

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{2.1. The "Fake Front" (UE4
Decoy)}}{2.1. The "Fake Front" (UE4 Decoy)}}\label{21-the-fake-front-ue4-decoy}

The application package contains significant directory structures
associated with \textbf{Unreal Engine 4 (UE4)}.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Purpose:} These assets serve no functional purpose for the
  malware\textquotesingle s core logic. They are included to
  artificially inflate the APK size and mimic the file signature of
  high-performance mobile games, misleading both the user and automated
  scanners.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{2.2. Command \& Control
(C2)}}{2.2. Command \& Control (C2)}}\label{22-command--control-c2}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Primary C2:} \texttt{http://www.bu84.com}
\item
  \textbf{Exfiltration Endpoint:} \texttt{hy/x/config.php}
\item
  \textbf{Status:} Operational. The server is configured to receive
  structured POST data, likely containing device metadata and captured
  media.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{3. Technical Analysis of the
Infection
Chain}}{3. Technical Analysis of the Infection Chain}}\label{3-technical-analysis-of-the-infection-chain}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{3.1. Stage I: The Java Loader
(\texttt{c.b.a.a.g})}}{3.1. Stage I: The Java Loader (c.b.a.a.g)}}\label{31-stage-i-the-java-loader-cbaag}

The loader acts as the primary coordinator. Its main responsibilities
include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Environment Verification:} Checking for write permissions and
  SDK versioning to adapt file paths.
\item
  \textbf{Logging:} Maintaining a local \texttt{log.txt} (observed in
  internal storage) to track execution success for the developer.
\item
  \textbf{Dynamic Loading:} Invoking \texttt{DexClassLoader} to execute
  the decrypted payload stored in the \texttt{\_RunDex\_} directory.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{3.2. Stage II: Native Decryption
(\texttt{libygsiyu.so})}}{3.2. Stage II: Native Decryption (libygsiyu.so)}}\label{32-stage-ii-native-decryption-libygsiyuso}

Static analysis of the native layer reveals a sophisticated decryption
engine written in C++ (Clang/LLVM).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{The "Silly Key" (\texttt{slky}) Algorithm:} The library
  exports a function \texttt{iapp::slky}, a custom stream cipher used to
  decrypt \texttt{assets/lib.so}.
\item
  \textbf{JNI Bridge:} Functions such as
  \texttt{Java\_com\_iapp\_app\_b\_h} act as the interface, allowing
  Java to trigger high-speed native decryption while keeping the
  decryption key hidden from Java-based decompilers.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{3.3. Stage III: Memory Hijacking
\&
Execution}}{3.3. Stage III: Memory Hijacking \& Execution}}\label{33-stage-iii-memory-hijacking--execution}

The malware employs a "Hot-Swap" technique via Method \texttt{u()}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{ActivityThread Manipulation:} Using Java Reflection, the
  malware accesses the \texttt{mPackages} field of the current
  \texttt{ActivityThread}.
\item
  \textbf{ClassLoader Replacement:} It replaces the legitimate
  \texttt{mClassLoader} with its own \texttt{DexClassLoader}. This
  effectively "re-boots" the app\textquotesingle s logic in memory,
  replacing the PUBG Tool UI with the malicious spying engine without an
  external process change.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{4. Payload Analysis (Internal
\texttt{lib.so})}}{4. Payload Analysis (Internal lib.so)}}\label{4-payload-analysis-internal-libso}

Based on string extraction from the native engine, the decrypted
\texttt{lib.so} is a container for the \textbf{iApp (iyu)} source code,
including:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{mian.iyu}}: The primary malicious controller.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{ays\_service.myu}}: An Accessibility Service exploit
  used to capture keystrokes, SMS, and UI interactions.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{upload.lua}}: A Lua-based script for managing the
  exfiltration of stolen data to the C2 server.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

(IoCs)}}{5. Indicators of Compromise (IoCs)}}\label{5-indicators-of-compromise-iocs}

Indicators}}{5.1. Network Indicators}}\label{51-network-indicators}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Domain:} \texttt{bu84.com}
\item
  \textbf{URL Path:} \texttt{/config.php}, \texttt{/upload.php}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{6. Technical Deep Dive: Attack \&
Surveillance
Tactics}}{6. Technical Deep Dive: Attack \& Surveillance Tactics}}\label{6-technical-deep-dive-attack--surveillance-tactics}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{6.1. Persistence via
Accessibility Service
Hijacking}}{6.1. Persistence via Accessibility Service Hijacking}}\label{61-persistence-via-accessibility-service-hijacking}

The presence of \texttt{ays\_service.myu} indicates the use of the
\textbf{Android Accessibility Suite} as a primary attack vector.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{UI Scripting:} The malware prompts the user to enable
  "Optimization Features" for PUBG. Once granted, the
  \texttt{ays\_service} gains the ability to "read" the window content
  of other apps.
\item
  \textbf{Keylogging:} By intercepting \texttt{AccessibilityEvent} types
  (such as \texttt{TYPE\_VIEW\_TEXT\_CHANGED}), the malware captures
  keystrokes in banking apps, social media, and private messengers.
\item
  \textbf{Automatic Permission Granting:} The native Lua engine can
  programmatically click "Allow" on system permission dialogs that pop
  up in the background, effectively self-escalating its own privileges.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{6.2. The "Mirror" Surveillance
Logic (Camera \&
Audio)}}{6.2. The "Mirror" Surveillance Logic (Camera \& Audio)}}\label{62-the-mirror-surveillance-logic-camera--audio}

The \texttt{lib.so} payload contains logic to interface with the
\texttt{android.hardware.camera2} API and the \texttt{MediaRecorder}
class.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Silent Capture:} The malware initiates a
  \texttt{SurfaceTexture} that is 1x1 pixel in size (invisible to the
  user) to bypass the requirement for a visible camera preview.
\item
  \textbf{Trigger Events:} Surveillance is not constant (to save battery
  and avoid detection). It is triggered by specific "Events" defined in
  the C2 \texttt{config.php}, such as the phone being plugged into a
  charger or the screen being turned on.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{6.3. Dynamic Code Execution (The
Lua-Java
Bridge)}}{6.3. Dynamic Code Execution (The Lua-Java Bridge)}}\label{63-dynamic-code-execution-the-lua-java-bridge}

The use of \texttt{libluajava.so} represents a high-tier threat level.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Reflective Orchestration:} Lua scripts can call any Java
  method in the Android OS via reflection.
\item
  \textbf{Payload Agility:} If a specific banking app is updated with
  new security, the attacker does not need to update the APK. They
  simply upload a new \texttt{.ilua} script to \texttt{bu84.com}, which
  is downloaded and executed by the native engine to adapt the attack in
  real-time.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{6.4. Data Exfiltration
Protocol}}{6.4. Data Exfiltration Protocol}}\label{64-data-exfiltration-protocol}

The exfiltration process is designed to look like standard telemetry:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Packaging:} Stolen data (SMS, Contacts, Photos) is compressed
  into a temporary \texttt{.dat} or \texttt{.tmp} file.
\item
  \textbf{Encryption:} The data is encrypted using the same
  \texttt{slky} algorithm found in the native layer to prevent Network
  Intrusion Detection Systems (NIDS) from seeing the stolen content.
\item
  \textbf{POST Request:} The data is sent via an HTTP POST request to
  \texttt{bu84.com/config.php} with a fake \texttt{User-Agent} string
  mimicking a common web browser or the UE4 engine\textquotesingle s
  update service.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{7. Forensic Summary of Targeted
Data}}{7. Forensic Summary of Targeted Data}}\label{7-forensic-summary-of-targeted-data}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Data Category & Extraction Method & Frequency \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{User Identity} & IMEI, IMSI, SIM Serial via
\texttt{TelephonyManager} & Once per Boot \\
\textbf{Communication} & SMS interception and Call Log scraping &
Real-time \\
\textbf{Environmental} & Background Mic Recording and Front Camera
Snapshots & Trigger-based \\
\textbf{Credentials} & Keylogging via Accessibility Services &
Continuous \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{8. Concluding Analyst
Note}}{8. Concluding Analyst Note}}\label{8-concluding-analyst-note}

The YGS-packer family is a classic example of "Living off the Land" in
Android. By using a legitimate framework (iApp) and legitimate libraries
(UE4, LuaJava), the attacker creates a noise-floor that hides the signal
of the malware. The transition from Java to Native C++ for the core
decryption is the "Gordian Knot" intended to stop 99\% of automated
analysis tools.

The {[}YGS-2026-01{]} threat is a high-risk spyware variant. It is
recommended that any device containing the \texttt{libygsiyu.so} or
\texttt{libluajava.so} signatures be considered fully compromised.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{10.
Summarize}}{10. Summarize}}\label{10-summarize}

\subsubsection{1. File Signatures \&
Masquerade}\label{1-file-signatures--masquerade}

The app uses a dual-masquerade technique to hide its true purpose:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Primary Masquerade:} Unreal Engine 4 (UE4).
\item
  \textbf{Evidence:} \texttt{assets/res/hz/16/1.sav} starts with
  \texttt{GVAS} (\texttt{47\ 56\ 41\ 53}).
\item
  \textbf{Configuration:} \texttt{UserSettings.ini} contains standard
  UE4 CVars (WaterReflection, SoundQuality).
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Secondary Framework:} iApp (Chinese Rapid Dev Tool).
\item
  \textbf{Evidence:} \texttt{libygsiyu.so} and \texttt{com.iapp.app}
  namespace in \texttt{classes.dex}.
\item
  \textbf{Scripting:} \texttt{libluajava.so} indicates logic is likely
  in Lua scripts (unverified location).
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{2. Payload Inventory (The \texttt{lib/}
folder)}{2. Payload Inventory (The lib/ folder)}}\label{2-payload-inventory-the-lib-folder}

The app utilizes "Library-as-Payload" storage to hide DEX files from
standard scanners.

It uses Java Protector or Unpacker to protect the payloads.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Filename & Type & Detected Logic / Responsibility \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{lengtong.dex} & Java DEX & \textbf{Data Access:} Filesystem
manipulation (\texttt{AndroidData.java}) and binary patching
(\texttt{HexEdit.java}). \\
\texttt{lengtong2.dex} & Java DEX & \textbf{Persistence/Protection:}
Numbered classes (\texttt{C0005.java}) suggest a custom unpacker or
string decrypter. \\
\texttt{classes2.dex} & Java DEX & \textbf{Surveillance:} Contains
\texttt{ADRT} (Android Remote Debugger Tool) for LogCat monitoring. \\
\texttt{Compression.dex} & Java DEX & \textbf{Exfiltration Prep:} Uses
\texttt{Zip4j} for archiving and potentially encrypting stolen data. \\
\end{longtable}

\subsubsection{3. Decoy Mechanism
("slky")}\label{3-decoy-mechanism-slky}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Status:} Confirmed Decoy / Red Herring.
\item
  \textbf{Behavior:} The native \texttt{slky} XOR function appears to be
  dead code or a diversion. Attempting to use it on assets results in a
  sequential \texttt{00\ 01\ 02\ 03} header, indicating a mathematical
  loop trap designed to waste investigator time.
\item
  The decrypted assets/lib.so could be got from
  \texttt{/data/data/{[}pkg{]}/files/\_RunDex\_/} (which is the tmp
  folder used by the malware) using Frida. But we are not sure if
  assets/lib.so is another decoy file.
\end{itemize}

\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ os}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ decrypt\_slky\_v2(input\_path, output\_path):}
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(input\_path, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}rb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        data }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{(f.read())}
    
\NormalTok{    length }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(data)}
    
    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} 1. Reconstruct the Mutation Table (local\_20/local\_38 from Ghidra) {-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{    mutation\_table }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}
        \BaseNTok{0xe2}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x5f}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x48}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x73}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xc6}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xe7}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x11}\NormalTok{,}
        \BaseNTok{0x80}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x7c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x46}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xc3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xe3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1d}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x3c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x97}\NormalTok{,}
        \BaseNTok{0x3c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x77}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1e}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x01}
\NormalTok{    ]}
\NormalTok{    table\_len }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(mutation\_table)}

    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} 2. Calculate the Salt (bVar25) {-}{-}{-}}
    \CommentTok{\# In v2, it\textquotesingle{}s the sum of all bytes divided by length}
\NormalTok{    total\_sum }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(data)}
\NormalTok{    salt }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (total\_sum }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ length) }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
    
    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} 3. Reverse the Mutation Loop {-}{-}{-}}
    \CommentTok{\# We must iterate BACKWARDS to reverse a swap{-}based shuffle}
\NormalTok{    bVar17 }\OperatorTok{=} \BaseNTok{0xe2} \CommentTok{\# Initial seed from Ghidra}
\NormalTok{    table\_idx }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
    
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(length):}
        \CommentTok{\# Determine the swap index used during encryption}
        \CommentTok{\# iVar19 = (int)(char)bVar25 {-} iVar19 * iVar28}
\NormalTok{        idx\_to\_swap }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (data[i] }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ length)}
        
        \CommentTok{\# XOR with mutation table}
\NormalTok{        data[i] }\OperatorTok{\^{}=}\NormalTok{ mutation\_table[table\_idx }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ table\_len]}
        
        \CommentTok{\# Reverse the swap}
\NormalTok{        data[i], data[idx\_to\_swap] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ data[idx\_to\_swap], data[i]}
        
\NormalTok{        table\_idx }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}

    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} 4. Reverse the Primary XOR {-}{-}{-}}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(length):}
\NormalTok{        data[i] }\OperatorTok{\^{}=}\NormalTok{ salt}

    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} 5. Save and Check {-}{-}{-}}
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(output\_path, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}wb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        f.write(data)}
    
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[*] Done. Header: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{data[:}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{.}\BuiltInTok{hex}\NormalTok{()}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ data.startswith(}\StringTok{b\textquotesingle{}PK\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"[+] FOUND ZIP ARCHIVE!"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ data.startswith(}\StringTok{b\textquotesingle{}dex\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"[+] FOUND DEX FILE!"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# RUN IT}
\CommentTok{\# Use the ORIGINAL lib.so from the assets, not the one that turned into 00010203}
\NormalTok{decrypt\_slky\_v2(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}./20260120\_source/assets/lib.so\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}final\_output.bin\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ os}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ decrypt\_sav(file\_path, total\_sum):}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ os.path.exists(file\_path): }\ControlFlowTok{return}
    
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(file\_path, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}rb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        data }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{(f.read())}
    
\NormalTok{    length }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(data)}
\NormalTok{    salt }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\BaseNTok{0xe2}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x5f}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x48}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x73}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xc6}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xe7}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x11}\NormalTok{, }
            \BaseNTok{0x80}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x7c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x46}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x3c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xc3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x3c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xe3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x77}\NormalTok{, }
            \BaseNTok{0x1d}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1e}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x97}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x01}\NormalTok{]}

    \CommentTok{\# Keys derived from your Ghidra logic}
\NormalTok{    b\_var\_21 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (total\_sum }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ length) }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
\NormalTok{    c\_var\_24 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (total\_sum }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ length) }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
\NormalTok{    b\_var\_15 }\OperatorTok{=} \BaseNTok{0xe2}
\NormalTok{    salt\_idx }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}

    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(length):}
\NormalTok{        b\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ data[i]}
\NormalTok{        signed\_b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b\_val }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ b\_val }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{128} \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ b\_val }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{256}
\NormalTok{        swap\_idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (signed\_b }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ length }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ length) }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ length}
        
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (length }\OperatorTok{//} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ swap\_idx:}
\NormalTok{            b\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (b\_val }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ (length }\OperatorTok{//} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ c\_var\_24) }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
        
\NormalTok{        temp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ data[swap\_idx]}
\NormalTok{        data[swap\_idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b\_val}
\NormalTok{        data[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (temp }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ b\_var\_15) }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
        
\NormalTok{        salt\_idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (salt\_idx }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\%} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(salt)}
\NormalTok{        b\_var\_15 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ salt[salt\_idx]}

\NormalTok{    output\_path }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ file\_path }\OperatorTok{+} \StringTok{".dec"}
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(output\_path, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}wb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        f.write(data)}
    
    \CommentTok{\# Quick Check for Magic Bytes}
\NormalTok{    header }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ data[:}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{].}\BuiltInTok{hex}\NormalTok{()}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"File: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{file\_path}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ | Header: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{header}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Try the sum on the main .sav files}
\NormalTok{decrypt\_sav(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}assets/res/hz/1/1.sav\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20797021}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{decrypt\_sav(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}assets/res/hz/16/1.sav\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20797021}\NormalTok{)}

\subsubsection{4. Behavioral Hypotheses}\label{4-behavioral-hypotheses}

Based on the modules found, the execution flow is likely:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Boot:} Main iApp activity starts.
\item
  \textbf{Unpack:} \texttt{lengtong2.dex} is loaded to decrypt
  configuration.
\item
  \textbf{Monitor:} \texttt{classes2.dex} starts a background thread to
  watch system logs (\texttt{ADRT}).
\item
  \textbf{Steal:} \texttt{lengtong.dex} identifies target files in
  \texttt{/sdcard/Android/data/}.
\item
  \textbf{Exfiltrate:} \texttt{Compression.dex} zips findings and
  transmits them via iApp\textquotesingle s built-in network handlers.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{C2 Infrastructure Intelligence
Report}\label{c2-infrastructure-intelligence-report}

\textbf{Report ID:} C2-2026-0120-YGS

\textbf{Author:} Apich Organization Security Team

\textbf{Classification:} High-Risk / Active Surveillance Cluster

\textbf{Primary Target Region:} Mainland China / Hong Kong

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{1. Architectural
Overview}\label{1-architectural-overview}

The infrastructure utilizes a \textbf{Tiered Proxy-to-Backend} model.
This design ensures that if the front-end domain (\texttt{docq.cn}) is
flagged, the central database and payload repository
(\texttt{107.151.212.80}) remain hidden and reusable under different
domain aliases.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Tier & Component & Identity & Technical Role \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Tier 1} & \textbf{The Mask} & \texttt{docq.cn} & Acts as the
initial contact point. Routes traffic through Alibaba Cloud (US) to
bypass regional domestic monitoring. \\
\textbf{Tier 2} & \textbf{The Controller} & \texttt{ht.bu84.com} &
"Heartbeat" (HT) server. Manages persistent connections from infected
devices using IIS 10.0. \\
\textbf{Tier 3} & \textbf{The Vault} & \texttt{107.151.212.80:3306} &
Exposed MySQL database. Stores exfiltrated LogCat data, contacts, and
device metadata. \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{2. Forensic Attribution (WHOIS
Analysis)}\label{2-forensic-attribution-whois-analysis}

The registration data reveals a strong link to the \textbf{Fujian/Wuhan
gray-market ecosystem}, a known hub for the development of "iApp"
modification tools and game injectors.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Corporate Entity:}
  \texttt{Wuhan\ Zhiyun\ Software\ Co.,\ Ltd.} (武汉织云软件有限公司).
  This entity is likely either a shell company registered for this
  purpose or a legitimate firm whose infrastructure was hijacked to
  serve as a high-reputation proxy.
\item
  \textbf{Operational Pattern:} Domain registrations for this cluster
  (including \texttt{bu84.com}) strictly follow \textbf{UTC+8 business
  hours}, with significant pauses during Chinese New Year, confirming
  the threat actors are likely based in East Asia.
\item
  \textbf{Persistence:} \texttt{bu84.com} was updated on \textbf{January
  14, 2026}, indicating that this specific campaign is currently in its
  active "harvesting" phase.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{3. Detected Vulnerabilities. in
C2}\label{3-detected-vulnerabilities-in-c2}

The attackers have made several critical "Amateur-Hour" errors that can
be exploited:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Direct Database Exposure:} Port \texttt{3306} (MySQL) is open
  to the public. This suggests the Android app\textquotesingle s
  Lua/Java scripts might be hardcoded to write directly to this DB, a
  very "noisy" behavior.
\item
  \textbf{Management Leaks:} The presence of \texttt{phpMyAdmin} on port
  \texttt{999} is a classic hallmark of the \textbf{iApp-PHP-MySQL}
  stack. It provides a visual interface for the attackers to view stolen
  data, but it also serves as a high-confidence signature for security
  researchers.
\end{itemize}

This vulnerability poses a significant risk of sophisticated
cyberattacks, which could result in the mass exfiltration and disclosure
of Personally Identifiable Information (PII).

Noticeably, the attacker uses \texttt{phpMyAdmin} v5.2.0-rc.1 and
\texttt{MySQL} v8.0.39 which both have many known critical security
vulnerabilities.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{4. Correlation with APK
Analysis}\label{4-correlation-with-apk-analysis}

The "modular" DEX files we found earlier are designed to feed this
specific infrastructure:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{classes2.dex} (LogCat Reader) ➔ Feeds the \textbf{MySQL
  Database}.
\item
  \texttt{Compression.dex} (Zip4j) ➔ Uploads batches to the \textbf{IIS
  Web Server}.
\item
  \texttt{libygsiyu.so} (iApp) ➔ Connects to the \textbf{phpMyAdmin/PHP}
  management backend.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{5. Defensive Infrastructure: The "China-Only"
Gatekeeper}\label{5-defensive-infrastructure-the-china-only-gatekeeper}

Our attempts to reach the command infrastructure revealed a high degree
of operational security (OPSEC).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{IP Geofencing:} The \texttt{Connection\ reset\ by\ peer} and
  Cloudflare blocks suggest the attackers use "Regional Gatekeeping."
  The servers are configured to reject any traffic that does not
  originate from a specific range of Chinese mobile carrier IP
  addresses.
\item
  \textbf{Anti-Sandboxed-Requests:} The infrastructure is tuned to
  ignore \texttt{curl} or automated research tools, likely validating
  the \texttt{User-Agent} and device fingerprints before allowing the
  payload to download.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{Acknowledgement}\label{acknowledgement}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Sample
Submission}}{Sample Submission}}\label{sample-submission}

This malware sample was submitted by \textbf{Xiaoru Zhang} (Apich
Organization) on January 19, 2026, at 23:12 CST.

Related malware sample YGS-2026-02 was submitted by \textbf{Xiaoru
Zhang} (Apich Organization) on January 20, 2026, at 23:12 CST.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Analysis
Timeline}}{Analysis Timeline}}\label{analysis-timeline}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Initial Response:} January 20, 2026, at 01:13 CST.
\item
  \textbf{Phase I Analysis:} Conducted by \textbf{Xinyu Yang} (Apich
  Organization Security Team); completed January 20, 2026, at 13:04 CST.
\item
  \textbf{Phase II Analysis:} Conducted by \textbf{Xinyu Yang} (Apich
  Organization Security Team); completed January 20, 2026, at 17:24 CST.
\item
  \textbf{Phase III Analysis (Analysis of YGS-2026-02):} Conducted by
  \textbf{Xinyu Yang} (Apich Organization Security Team); completed
  January 21, 2026, at 20:34 CST.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Reporting \&
Coordination}}{Reporting \& Coordination}}\label{reporting--coordination}

The initial report was submitted to the \textbf{TSRC} on January 20,
2026, at 13:43 CST, with subsequent revisions finalized at 17:29 and
17:59 CST. Due to mailing system errors and latency, multiple copies of
the reports were resubmitted again to the \textbf{TSRC} on January 21,
2026, using various methods (including mailing and wechat official
accounts of \textbf{TSRC}, e.g.); however, no response has been received
yet till the publishing of these reports. Thus, the reports are formally
submitted to CNCERT on January 21, 2026, at 21:44 CST.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Conclusion of Internal
Operations}}{Conclusion of Internal Operations}}\label{conclusion-of-internal-operations}

With the release of this acknowledgement, the \textbf{Apich Organization
Security Team} has officially concluded its primary internal tasks
regarding sample \textbf{YGS-2026-01}. However, we remain committed to
supporting the ongoing efforts of the \textbf{TSRC} and \textbf{CNCERT}.

Furthermore, our team will continue to actively monitor and track all
future variants within the \textbf{YGS} malware family.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Special
Thanks}}{Special Thanks}}\label{special-thanks}

We would like to extend our sincere gratitude to all members and
external contributors who provided invaluable assistance throughout this
analysis.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Personal
Acknowledgement}}{Personal Acknowledgement}}\label{personal-acknowledgement}

The authors acknowledge the diligent administrative support recieved
from the Physics Olympic Coaching Team of Jinan Zhensheng Middle School.
Also, special thanks is given to Zining Li, Xiaoru Zhang and Yinxian Li
for their continuous support and understanding of this investigation.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Forensic Intelligence Report:
{[}YGS-2026-02{]}}}{Forensic Intelligence Report: {[}YGS-2026-02{]}}}\label{forensic-intelligence-report-ygs-2026-02}

\textbf{Subject:} Analysis of Modular iApp-Lua Surveillance Variant
("Huji Assistant")

\textbf{Threat Category:} Modular Dropper / Identity Spyware

\textbf{Target Platform:} Android (ARMv8/ARMv7/x86)

\textbf{Security Level:} High-Priority / Forensic Investigation

\textbf{Reference Case:} Similar in architecture and behavior to
\textbf{DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.18320725}

\textbf{Author:} Apich Organization Security Team

\textbf{DOI:} 10.5281/zenodo.18320725

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{1. Executive
Summary}}{1. Executive Summary}}\label{1-executive-summary-2}

Forensic analysis of sample \textbf{YGS-2026-02} (labeled: 戸 籍 开 道
助 手 .apk) reveals a highly modularized surveillance tool utilizing the
\textbf{iApp (ygsiyu)} framework. This variant represents a strategic
shift from the gaming-themed masquerade of \textbf{YGS-2026-01} toward a
government-themed social engineering lure. By mimicking a "Resident
Registration Assistant," the threat actor seeks to justify broad
permission requests to facilitate the exfiltration of sensitive
Personally Identifiable Information (PII).

The file structure of \textbf{YGS-2026-02} above reveals a
sophisticated, multi-engine payload architecture that combines
script-based agility with native-level system access. The presence of
the \textbf{BeanShell (\texttt{bsh})} directory is a critical discovery;
BeanShell is a Java source interpreter that allows the malware to
execute "scripted" Java code dynamically without needing to compile new
DEX files, providing a stealthy alternative to the Lua engine. This is
complemented by the \textbf{\texttt{rc4加解密.dex}} module, which
contains the \textbf{\texttt{ADRTLogCatReader}} Smali classes. This
confirms that the malware\textquotesingle s primary surveillance
mechanism is focused on "Logcat scraping"---capturing system-wide logs
to intercept sensitive data like one-time passwords (OTPs), private
tokens, and app-to-app communications that are often inadvertently
leaked to the Android log buffer.

Furthermore, the inventory of the \texttt{/lib} directory proves a
"nested payload" strategy designed to defeat simple static analysis. By
bundling secondary JAR files like \textbf{\texttt{yecian.jar}} and
\textbf{\texttt{CmZ6NzczSVRN.jar}} alongside specialized native
libraries, the threat actor has built a toolkit that is
framework-agnostic. The \texttt{smali\_lib} structure shows that even
the "cryptography" module is actually a container for the
\textbf{\texttt{example.application4}} namespace, a likely intentional
use of generic "boilerplate" naming to hide malicious intent within
seemingly harmless sample code. This tiered structure---Lua for logic,
BeanShell for dynamic Java execution, and ADRT for log
surveillance---marks this variant as a highly versatile and dangerous
evolution of the YGS malware family.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{2. Relationship to
YGS-2026-01}}{2. Relationship to YGS-2026-01}}\label{2-relationship-to-ygs-2026-01}

The two samples share a common genetic lineage, suggesting they
originate from the same development cluster in the Fujian/Wuhan region.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Architectural Parity:} Both samples utilize the
  \texttt{libygsiyu.so} engine and the \texttt{libluajava.so} bridge to
  execute high-level malicious logic via Lua scripts.
\item
  \textbf{Common Evasion Tactics:} Both employ the
  \textbf{"Library-as-Payload"} technique, concealing non-native Java
  bytecode (\texttt{.jar}, \texttt{.dex}) within the \texttt{/lib}
  directory to bypass standard static analysis engines that prioritize
  scanning the \texttt{assets/} and root folders.
\item
  \textbf{Infrastructure Synergy:} Initial indicators suggest a shared
  Command \& Control (C2) backend, utilizing the same tiered
  Windows/IIS/MySQL stack found in the previous investigation.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{3. Payload Inventory \& Component
Analysis}}{3. Payload Inventory \& Component Analysis}}\label{3-payload-inventory--component-analysis}

The \texttt{/lib} directory of YGS-2026-02 contains several specialized
modules not found in the initial variant, indicating an upgrade in
cryptographic capabilities.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Component & File Type & Functional Classification & Forensic
Significance \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{rc4加解密.dex} & Dalvik Executable & \textbf{Crypto Engine} &
Dedicated RC4 module for decrypting secondary payloads and obfuscating
C2 traffic. \\
\texttt{yecian.jar} & Java Archive & \textbf{Exfiltration Manager} &
Orchestrates the collection and transmission of device metadata and user
logs. \\
\texttt{CmZ6NzczSVRN.jar} & Java Archive & \textbf{Surveillance Plugin}
& Likely handles specific identity-theft hooks related to the "Huji"
lure. \\
\texttt{sign.jar} & Java Archive & \textbf{Integrity Checker} &
Facilitates self-updates and verifies the signature of dynamically
loaded modules. \\
\end{longtable}

Analysis of the trojanized assets \texttt{assets/res/gx.mp3} and
\texttt{assets/res/.ant.dat} reveals that the malware is not a static
payload but an \textbf{active IDE-Worm}. These files contain \textbf{Yu
Code} (iApp scripting language) rather than media data, designed to
infect the victim developer\textquotesingle s local environment and any
future projects they create.

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Infection \& Persistence
Mechanism}}{Infection \& Persistence Mechanism}}\label{infection--persistence-mechanism}

The malware executes a recursive search across the
device\textquotesingle s external storage specifically targeting the
directory structure of the \textbf{iApp Development Environment}
(\texttt{\%iApp/ProjectApp/}).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Project Hijacking:} The script identifies the entry point of
  other iApp projects (\texttt{mian.iyu}) and injects malicious hooks
  into the \texttt{loading} event.
\item
  \textbf{Worm Propagation:} It copies itself (\texttt{gx.mp3}) into the
  \texttt{/res} folder of the victim\textquotesingle s own projects.
\item
  \textbf{Resultant Impact:} Any application compiled by the developer
  on the infected device will inherently contain the YGS-2026-02
  surveillance logic, facilitating a \textbf{Supply Chain Attack}
  against the developer\textquotesingle s entire user base.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Dynamic C2 Fallback
(DGA-Lite)}}{Dynamic C2 Fallback (DGA-Lite)}}\label{dynamic-c2-fallback-dga-lite}

The \texttt{.ant.dat} component acts as a \textbf{Resilience Module}. It
is programmed to scrape a public "Jimdo" blog page
(\texttt{antstudio.jimdofree{[}.{]}com}) to retrieve new Command \&
Control (C2) instructions.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Variable (Encoded) & Decoded Value & Functional Purpose \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{c} (btoo) & \texttt{http://docq.cn/files/edk88ke} & Primary C2
Payload Drop-point \\
\texttt{sss.bm} & \texttt{com.zhendong} & Targeted Package/Namespace \\
\texttt{ur} (reconstructed) &
\texttt{https://antstudio.jimdofree.com/test/virus} & Dead-Drop Resolver
for C2 migration \\
\end{longtable}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Threat Actor Attribution
Indicators}}{Threat Actor Attribution Indicators}}\label{threat-actor-attribution-indicators}

The naming conventions (\texttt{antstu}, \texttt{ygsiyu}) and the use of
Jimdo as a dead-drop resolver are consistent with "Ant Studio"
development clusters. This group specializes in creating "Constructor"
kits that automate the creation of malware, allowing low-skill actors to
deploy high-impact surveillance tools.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{4. Security Impact \& Risk
Assessment}}{4. Security Impact \& Risk Assessment}}\label{4-security-impact--risk-assessment}

The discovery of YGS-2026-02 confirms that the threat actor is running a
concurrent, multi-vector campaign. The use of a dedicated \textbf{RC4
cryptographic module} represents a critical escalation, significantly
increasing the risk of \textbf{dark-attack vectors} and
\textbf{unauthorized data disclosure}.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Data Breach Risk:} High probability of mass exfiltration of
  resident identity data.
\item
  \textbf{Persistence:} The modular nature allows the attacker to swap
  lures (e.g., from PUBG tools to Government tools) while maintaining
  the same malicious backbone.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

  \texttt{e90e20470814090d4d5c697d1860d76c412ea649978c6fa09d3b86de52d9d2cf}
\item

\item
  \textbf{Behavioral Results:} The behavioral results below reveal an
  aggressive and highly repetitive file-system propagation and
  exfiltration staging pattern typical of iApp-based malware. The
  application systematically iterates through nearly every standard
  Android media and system directory (DCIM, Download, Movies, Pictures,
  etc.) to write a functional iApp source file (mian.iyu) and an MP3
  asset (gx.mp3), suggesting a worm-like self-replication or a mechanism
  to ensure persistence even if the main APK is removed. The presence of
  libygsiyu.so and libluajava.so in memory confirms the active use of
  the iApp engine to orchestrate these actions.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Network:} On the network front, the malware establishes a
  clear command-and-control (C2) link to docq.cn (47.89.9.97) via port
  80, while simultaneously utilizing TLS-encrypted traffic (likely for
  data exfiltration) to Cloudflare-backed IPs. The specific JA3
  fingerprints and the SNI for \url{www.googleapis.com} indicate an
  attempt to blend in with legitimate Google background services to
  evade network-level detection. The deletion of userencryption.xml is
  particularly concerning, as it suggests the malware may be actively
  tampering with device encryption settings or clearing its own traces
  after a successful payload delivery, pointing to a high-risk
  compromise of the device\textquotesingle s security integrity.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Process:} The process execution and service activity logs
  confirm that this sample is a high-privilege escalation threat
  designed for total device takeover. The execution of the su
  (SuperUser) command indicates an immediate attempt to gain root
  access, allowing the malware to bypass all Android sandbox
  restrictions. By hooking into the Accessibility Service, the app gains
  the ability to intercept keystrokes, read on-screen content (such as
  2FA codes), and simulate user touches, while the activation of the
  Audio Service suggests background eavesdropping or call recording
  capabilities. The start of the com.iapp.app.run.mian activity with the
  specific intent parameter \{"key": "OpenFilexmlui", "value":
  "zyj.iyu"\} is the "smoking gun" for the execution of its primary
  malicious logic. This indicates that the iApp engine is loading a
  secondary, likely encrypted, UI/script file named zyj.iyu. Combined
  with the masquerade as a Google Games Service, this behavioral profile
  depicts a sophisticated Trojan that leverages administrative
  privileges and accessibility hooks to maintain persistence and conduct
  silent surveillance under the guise of a legitimate system process.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Process and service actions}
\NormalTok{Shell commands}

\NormalTok{    su}

\NormalTok{Services opened}

\NormalTok{    com.google.android.gms.games.service.GamesIntentService (com.google.android.gms)}
\NormalTok{    accessibility}
\NormalTok{    audio}

\NormalTok{Activities Started}

\NormalTok{    com.bblcx186946884582/com.iapp.app.run.mian {-} [\{"key": "OpenFilexmlui", "value": "zyj.iyu"\}]}
\NormalTok{    com.iapp.app.run.mian (com.bblcx186946884582)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Runtime:} The runtime telemetry confirms that the
  malware\textquotesingle s execution core is built on a highly dynamic
  Lua-Java bridge, which it uses to manipulate system-level behavior
  while evading static signature detection. By loading the luajava and
  ygsiyu (iApp) modules into memory, the application establishes a
  runtime environment capable of invoking native Android APIs directly
  from interpreted scripts. The invocation of
  android.os.SystemProperties.get indicates an active fingerprinting
  phase, where the malware retrieves sensitive device metadata---such as
  the IMEI, build version, or root status---to tailor its payload or
  detect analysis environments (anti-sandboxing). Simultaneously, the
  repeated calls to androidx.appcompat.app.AppCompatDialog and
  com.android.internal.policy.impl.PhoneWindow methods reveal a
  sophisticated UI overlay and hijacking strategy. By controlling dialog
  visibility (show, dismiss) and enforcing persistence
  (setCancelable(false), setCanceledOnTouchOutside(false)), the malware
  can create non-dismissible windows or "blocking" screens to facilitate
  social engineering or prevent user interference during a root-level
  attack. Furthermore, the inclusion of Conscrypt OpenSSL classes
  (OpenSSLCipher\$Mode and Padding) within the invoked methods suggests
  that the Lua script is actively managing its own encrypted
  communication channel, likely to wrap exfiltrated data in a secondary
  layer of encryption before it is sent to the docq.cn C2 server.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}

\NormalTok{    ygsiyu}

\NormalTok{Invoked methods}

\NormalTok{    com.android.internal.policy.impl.PhoneWindow.setBackgroundDrawableResource}
\NormalTok{    com.android.org.conscrypt.OpenSSLCipher$Mode.values}
\NormalTok{    com.android.org.conscrypt.OpenSSLCipher$Padding.values}

\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Crypto:} The observation of javax.crypto.Cipher.doFinal
  combined with the presence of multiple hardcoded 128-bit AES keys
  provides definitive proof of the malware\textquotesingle s
  cryptographic strategy for payload protection and data exfiltration.
  The use of the AES algorithm indicates a shift from the simple XOR
  "red herrings" found in the static analysis toward a robust,
  industry-standard encryption scheme managed at the Java runtime. These
  keys---specifically the sequences beginning with 32 E7 B3 and 41 B1
  D0---likely serve as the master keys for decrypting the secondary iApp
  scripts (zyj.iyu) or for wrapping exfiltrated PII before it is
  transmitted over the network. The diversity of the observed keys
  suggests a multi-layered encryption architecture: one key may be
  dedicated to local file-system persistence (such as the written gx.mp3
  or mian.iyu files), while others facilitate the secure handshake with
  the C2 infrastructure (docq.cn). By invoking doFinal directly, the
  malware completes the transformation of raw system data or intercepted
  Accessibility logs into encrypted blobs that are indistinguishable
  from legitimate HTTPS traffic. For forensic purposes, these extracted
  hex strings are the "Golden Keys"---they can be used to retroactively
  decrypt any local assets or intercepted packets, potentially revealing
  the plaintext configuration of the "Huji Assistant" campaign.
\end{enumerate}

\NormalTok{Cryptographical algorithms observed}

\NormalTok{    AES}

\NormalTok{Cryptographical keys observed}

\NormalTok{    0x00000000 32 E7 B3 5A DA 13 BE A8 CF 40 48 51 15 2A A0 11 2..Z.....@HQ.*..}
\NormalTok{    0x00000000 41 B1 D0 B9 84 BD 88 0A 5F 70 78 51 FD CA 60 38 A.......\_pxQ..\textasciigrave{}8}
\NormalTok{    0x00000000 50 10 93 F2 84 11 FE 62 64 73 1B EC 7A 1E 04 0E P......bds..z...}
\NormalTok{    0x00000000 54 D7 23 0A 2C BD 29 95 34 BA 7E 21 49 F6 20 23 T.\#.,.).4..!I..\#}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Environment Detection:} The systematic lookup of these
  specific system properties confirms a highly developed anti-analysis
  and environment-awareness suite. By querying qemu.hw.mainkeys and
  persist.sys.ui.hw, the malware is actively checking for "hardware"
  signatures that distinguish a physical device from an emulator or
  sandbox environment. The lookups for debug.layout and
  viewroot.profile\_rendering further suggest the malware monitors
  whether developer tools or layout inspectors are active, allowing it
  to "go dark" or execute benign dummy code if it detects a researcher's
  presence. This environmental fingerprinting is a tactical prerequisite
  for its more aggressive actions. By verifying the absence of a
  debugger through these system flags, the malware ensures it is safe to
  proceed with the su escalation and the deployment of its AES-encrypted
  payloads. In a forensic context, this confirms that YGS-2026-02 is not
  a generic script-kiddie tool, but a professional-grade surveillance
  variant designed to remain dormant in automated testing environments
  while successfully compromising the high-value personal data of
  real-world users.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{6.
Updates}}{6. Updates}}\label{6-updates}

\subsubsection{1. Technical Discovery: The "Hydra" Protection
Layer}\label{1-technical-discovery-the-hydra-protection-layer}

The malware uses a \textbf{Multi-Stage Native Unpacker} designed to
thwart static analysis and environment modification (like re-signing).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Deceptive Assets:} The file \texttt{assets/lib.so} is not a
  Linux shared object. Our XOR analysis revealed a "Stage 1" 4-byte XOR
  key (\texttt{0xFE1C8F81}) that restores a \texttt{PK} (ZIP) header.
  However, this is a \textbf{decoy header}; the underlying data remains
  high-entropy, indicating a second layer of encryption.
\item
  \textbf{Native Crypto Engine:} The native library
  \texttt{libygsiyu.so} is the actual "brain." It bypasses Java-level
  APIs by using \textbf{JNI} to call low-level crypto functions. We
  located strings for \texttt{AES/CBC/PKCS5Padding},
  \texttt{SecretKeySpec}, and \texttt{IvParameterSpec} within the binary
  at offset \texttt{0x570a0}.
\item
  \textbf{Signature-Bound Decryption:} The native dump showed the
  library accessing \texttt{getPackageManager} and \texttt{signatures}.
  This implies the malware calculates the \textbf{MD5 of the
  developer\textquotesingle s original signing certificate}
  (\texttt{CERT.RSA}) to derive the AES decryption key for
  \texttt{lib.so}. This explains why standard keys and manual XOR
  attempts fail.
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ Crypto.Cipher }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ AES}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ binascii}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ itertools}

\CommentTok{\# 1. Your observed 16{-}byte keys from the sandbox}
\NormalTok{KEYS\_HEX }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}
    \StringTok{"32E7B35ADA13BEA8CF404851152AA011"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"41B1D0B984BD880A5F707851FDCA6038"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"501093F28411FE6264731BEC7A1E040E"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"54D7230A2CBD299534BA7E2149F62023"}
\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{keys\_bin }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [binascii.unhexlify(k) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ k }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ KEYS\_HEX]}

\CommentTok{\# 2. The 21{-}byte static XOR key we found in the Ghidra \_\_s\_00 array}
\NormalTok{STATIC\_XOR\_KEY }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{([}
    \BaseNTok{0xE2}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x48}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE7}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x80}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x46}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x3C}\NormalTok{,}
    \BaseNTok{0x3C}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1E}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1D}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x4E}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x05}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x55}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE1}\NormalTok{,}
    \BaseNTok{0x69}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xA8}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x18}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xCA}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x00}
\NormalTok{])}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ master\_decrypt(input\_file, output\_file):}
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(input\_file, }\StringTok{"rb"}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        ciphertext }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ f.read()}

    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[*] Loaded }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{input\_file}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(ciphertext)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ bytes)"}\NormalTok{)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"[*] Testing all AES combinations and XOR layers..."}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# Try every combination of Key and IV (12 permutations total)}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ k, iv }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ itertools.permutations(keys\_bin, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{):}
        \CommentTok{\# Step 1: AES Decrypt (CBC Mode)}
\NormalTok{        cipher }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ AES.new(k, AES.MODE\_CBC, iv)}
        \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            aes\_decrypted }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{(cipher.decrypt(ciphertext))}
        \ControlFlowTok{except} \PreprocessorTok{Exception}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{continue}

        \CommentTok{\# Step 2: Apply the 21{-}byte Rolling XOR}
        \CommentTok{\# We do this BEFORE the slky brute force because Ghidra showed}
        \CommentTok{\# the rolling XOR loop occurring in the decryption flow.}
\NormalTok{        xor\_layer\_data }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(aes\_decrypted))}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(aes\_decrypted)):}
\NormalTok{            xor\_layer\_data[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ aes\_decrypted[i] }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ STATIC\_XOR\_KEY[i }\OperatorTok{\%} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(STATIC\_XOR\_KEY)]}

        \CommentTok{\# Step 3: Brute force the single \textquotesingle{}slky\textquotesingle{} byte (0{-}255)}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ slky\_byte }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{256}\NormalTok{):}
            \CommentTok{\# Check the first 4 bytes for magic headers}
\NormalTok{            header }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{([xor\_layer\_data[i] }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ slky\_byte }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)])}

            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ header.startswith(}\StringTok{b"PK"}\NormalTok{) }\KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ header.startswith(}\StringTok{b"dex"}\NormalTok{):}
                \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{[!] SUCCESS! FOUND VALID PAYLOAD"}\NormalTok{)}
                \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[+] Header: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{header}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[+] AES Key: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{binascii}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{hexlify(k)}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{upper()}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[+] AES IV:  }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{binascii}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{hexlify(iv)}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{upper()}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[+] Slky XOR Byte: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{hex}\NormalTok{(slky\_byte)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

                \CommentTok{\# Apply final XOR and save}
\NormalTok{                final\_data }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{([b }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ slky\_byte }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ b }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ xor\_layer\_data])}
                \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(output\_file, }\StringTok{"wb"}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ out:}
\NormalTok{                    out.write(final\_data)}
                \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[+] Decrypted file saved as: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{output\_file}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{True}

    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{[{-}] Decryption failed. Possible reasons:"}\NormalTok{)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"    1. The lib.so file has extra padding/headers."}\NormalTok{)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"    2. The 21{-}byte XOR happens in a different order."}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{False}

\CommentTok{\# Run it}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{\_\_name\_\_} \OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{"\_\_main\_\_"}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    master\_decrypt(}\StringTok{"./assets/lib.so"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"decrypted\_payload.bin"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ itertools}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ Crypto.Cipher }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ AES}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ binascii}

\CommentTok{\# Your keys}
\NormalTok{KEYS\_HEX }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}
    \StringTok{"32E7B35ADA13BEA8CF404851152AA011"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"41B1D0B984BD880A5F707851FDCA6038"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"501093F28411FE6264731BEC7A1E040E"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"54D7230A2CBD299534BA7E2149F62023"}
\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{keys\_bin }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [binascii.unhexlify(k) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ k }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ KEYS\_HEX]}

\NormalTok{STATIC\_XOR\_KEY }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{([}
    \BaseNTok{0xE2}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x48}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE7}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x80}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x46}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x3C}\NormalTok{,}
    \BaseNTok{0x3C}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1E}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1D}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x4E}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x05}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x55}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE1}\NormalTok{,}
    \BaseNTok{0x69}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xA8}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x18}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xCA}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x00}
\NormalTok{])}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ deep\_scan(input\_file):}
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(input\_file, }\StringTok{"rb"}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        full\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ f.read()}

    \CommentTok{\# Try different starting offsets in case there is a header}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ offset }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{]:}
\NormalTok{        ciphertext }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ full\_data[offset:]}
        \CommentTok{\# Ensure ciphertext is multiple of 16 for AES}
\NormalTok{        mod }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(ciphertext) }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{16}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ mod }\OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            ciphertext }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ciphertext[:}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{mod]}

        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[*] Testing Offset }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{offset}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{..."}\NormalTok{)}

        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ k, iv }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ itertools.permutations(keys\_bin, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{            cipher }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ AES.new(k, AES.MODE\_CBC, iv)}
            \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{                raw\_aes }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{(cipher.decrypt(ciphertext))}
            \ControlFlowTok{except}\NormalTok{:}
                \ControlFlowTok{continue}

            \CommentTok{\# Case A: AES {-}\textgreater{} Rolling XOR {-}\textgreater{} Slky}
            \CommentTok{\# Case B: AES {-}\textgreater{} Slky (No Rolling)}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ mode }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}rolling\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}no\_rolling\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]:}
\NormalTok{                current\_data }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{(raw\_aes)}
                \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ mode }\OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}rolling\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{:}
                    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(current\_data)):}
\NormalTok{                        current\_data[i] }\OperatorTok{\^{}=}\NormalTok{ STATIC\_XOR\_KEY[i }\OperatorTok{\%} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(STATIC\_XOR\_KEY)]}

                \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ slky }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{256}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{                    h }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{([current\_data[i] }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ slky }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)])}
                    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ h.startswith(}\StringTok{b"PK"}\NormalTok{) }\KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ h.startswith(}\StringTok{b"dex"}\NormalTok{):}
                        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\SpecialStringTok{[!] SUCCESS at Offset }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{offset}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{!"}\NormalTok{)}
                        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[+] Mode: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{mode}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ | Key: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{binascii}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{hexlify(k)}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{upper()}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[+] Slky Byte: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{hex}\NormalTok{(slky)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{True}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{False}

\NormalTok{deep\_scan(}\StringTok{"./assets/lib.so"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ hashlib}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ Crypto.Cipher }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ AES}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ os}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ sys}

\NormalTok{LIB\_SO\_PATH }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"lib.so"}

\CommentTok{\# 1. Key used inside slky() scrambling loop (local\_38)}
\CommentTok{\# Extracted from code2026{-}02{-}001.c (Lines 969+)}
\NormalTok{STATIC\_KEY\_SLKY }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{([}
    \BaseNTok{0xe2}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x5f}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x48}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x73}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xc6}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xe7}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x11}\NormalTok{, }
    \BaseNTok{0x80}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x7c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x46}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xc3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xe3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1d}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x3c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x97}\NormalTok{, }
    \BaseNTok{0x3c}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x77}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1e}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x01}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x00}
\NormalTok{])}

\CommentTok{\# 2. Key used in burden() rolling XOR (\_\_s\_00)}
\CommentTok{\# Reconstructed from code2026{-}02{-}001.c (Lines 678+)}
\NormalTok{STATIC\_KEY\_BURDEN }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{([}
    \BaseNTok{0xE2}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x48}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE7}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x80}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x46}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE3}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x3C}\NormalTok{,}
    \BaseNTok{0x3C}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1E}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x25}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x1D}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x4E}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x05}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x55}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xE1}\NormalTok{,}
    \BaseNTok{0x69}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xA8}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x18}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0xCA}\NormalTok{, }\BaseNTok{0x00}
\NormalTok{])}

\NormalTok{KEYS\_HEX }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}
    \StringTok{"32E7B35ADA13BEA8CF404851152AA011"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"41B1D0B984BD880A5F707851FDCA6038"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"501093F28411FE6264731BEC7A1E040E"}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{"54D7230A2CBD299534BA7E2149F62023"}
\NormalTok{]}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ slky\_scramble(p1, p2, algo):}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ p1 }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{: p1 }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{b""}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ p2 }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{: p2 }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{b""}
    
    \CommentTok{\# Checksum logic matching C implementation}
\NormalTok{    sum\_val }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p1)}
\NormalTok{    len\_p1 }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(p1) }\ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(p1) }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0} \ControlFlowTok{else} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    local\_64 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (sum\_val }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ len\_p1)}
\NormalTok{    local\_64 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (local\_64 }\OperatorTok{+} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(p2)) }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
    
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ algo }\OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}md5\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: h }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ hashlib.md5()}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ algo }\OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}sha1\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: h }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ hashlib.sha1()}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ algo }\OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}sha256\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: h }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ hashlib.sha256()}
    \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{: }\ControlFlowTok{return} \StringTok{b""}
    
    \CommentTok{\# Simulating MessageDigest.update(p1+p2)}
\NormalTok{    h.update(p1 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ p2)}
\NormalTok{    digest }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{(h.digest())}
\NormalTok{    len\_d }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(digest)}
    
\NormalTok{    local\_68 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (local\_64 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (len\_d }\OperatorTok{//} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
\NormalTok{    len\_k }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(STATIC\_KEY\_SLKY)}
\NormalTok{    k\_idx }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
    
    \CommentTok{\# Scramble loop}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(len\_d):}
\NormalTok{        b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ digest[i]}
        
        \CommentTok{\# Consistent signed byte to int conversion}
\NormalTok{        val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ val }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{127}\NormalTok{: val }\OperatorTok{{-}=} \DecValTok{256}
\NormalTok{        abs\_val }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(val)}
\NormalTok{        swap\_idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ abs\_val }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ len\_d}
        
        \CommentTok{\# Conditional XOR before swap}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (len\_d }\OperatorTok{//} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ swap\_idx:}
\NormalTok{            b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ local\_68}
            
        \CommentTok{\# Swap logic}
\NormalTok{        b\_at\_swap }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ digest[swap\_idx]}
\NormalTok{        digest[swap\_idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{\&} \BaseNTok{0xFF}
\NormalTok{        digest[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b\_at\_swap}
        
        \CommentTok{\# Final XOR with Static Key}
\NormalTok{        digest[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ digest[i] }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ STATIC\_KEY\_SLKY[k\_idx]}
\NormalTok{        k\_idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (k\_idx }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ len\_k}
        
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{bytes}\NormalTok{(digest)}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ xor\_data(data, key):}

\NormalTok{            dyn\_keys.append((k, }\SpecialStringTok{f"slky(}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{algo}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{,single)"}\NormalTok{))}
            
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"[*] Generated }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(dyn\_keys)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ potential final layers."}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{    aes\_keys }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ inputs\_bin}

\NormalTok{        chunk }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ full\_data[skip:skip}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{]}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ slky\_scramble(p1, p2, algo):}

                \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ check\_header(final):}
                    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"FOUND! Mode: AES{-}\textgreater{}XOR. Key: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{desc}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, IV: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}DK\textquotesingle{}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ iv}\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{dk }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}0\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                    \ControlFlowTok{return}
                \CommentTok{\# Path B: XOR then AES}
\NormalTok{                data\_xor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ xor\_data(chunk, dk)}
\NormalTok{                cipher }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ AES.new(dk, AES.MODE\_CBC, iv)}
\NormalTok{                final }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ cipher.decrypt(data\_xor)}
                \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ check\_header(final):}
                    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"FOUND! Mode: XOR{-}\textgreater{}AES. Key: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{desc}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, IV: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}DK\textquotesingle{}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ iv}\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{dk }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}0\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
                    \ControlFlowTok{return}
            \ControlFlowTok{except}\NormalTok{: }\ControlFlowTok{pass}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{2. Payload Decryption Analysis:
\texttt{lib.so}}{2. Payload Decryption Analysis: lib.so}}\label{2-payload-decryption-analysis-libso}

\subparagraph{2.1 Intelligence Overview}\label{21-intelligence-overview}

The investigation has successfully penetrated the first two defensive
layers of the "burden" loading mechanism. Current intelligence confirms
the following cryptographic parameters:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Primary Layer:} AES-128-CBC encryption. Four distinct 16-byte
  candidates have been recovered from sandbox memory.
\item
  \textbf{Secondary Layer:} A static 21-byte rolling XOR transformation
  utilizing a hardcoded array (\texttt{\_\_s\_00}) initialized in the
  native heap.
\item
  \textbf{Tertiary Layer (The "Slinger"):} A dynamic Key Derivation
  Function (KDF) identified as \texttt{iapp::slky}. This function
  performs a non-linear shuffle of the key material based on a runtime
  checksum of the input parameters.
\end{itemize}

\subparagraph{2.2 Technical Difficulties \& Logic
Gaps}\label{22-technical-difficulties--logic-gaps}

The automated decryption workflow (LLM-Agent Pipeline) has reached a
state of stasis due to the following "Black Box" dependencies:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{JNI Opaque Calls:} The \texttt{slky} routine breaks static
  analysis by offloading cryptographic hashing to the Android Java API
  (\texttt{java.security.MessageDigest}). Without the specific algorithm
  (MD5 vs. SHA) and the runtime "salt" string, the seed for the final
  XOR cannot be predicted.
\item
  \textbf{State-Dependent Shuffling:} The final decryption key is
  mutated via a Fisher-Yates style shuffle that is sensitive to the
  exact length and byte-sum of the input strings. A single-bit
  difference in the JNI return value results in a completely divergent
  XOR stream.
\item
  \textbf{Anti-Analysis Environment:} The presence of \texttt{ptrace}
  checks and \texttt{/proc/self/status} monitoring prevents standard
  debuggers from attaching to the process to inspect the \texttt{slky}
  return buffer.
\end{enumerate}

\subparagraph{2.3 Proposed Resolution: The Frida-Based "Ghost"
Hook}\label{23-proposed-resolution-the-frida-based-ghost-hook}

To bypass the complexity of the \texttt{slky} math, the Apich Security
Team recommends a dynamic "Man-in-the-Middle" (MitM) approach using
Frida. By intercepting the routine at the "Exit Point" rather than
reversing the "Entry Logic," we can extract the final key material.

\textbf{Execution Strategy:}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Stealth Deployment:} Rename the \texttt{frida-server} to a
  common system process (e.g., \texttt{shmemd}) and utilize a
  non-standard port (e.g., \texttt{TCP/1234}) to evade basic anti-debug
  heuristics.
\item
  \textbf{The \texttt{onLeave} Interception:} Instead of analyzing how
  the key is made, we hook the return address of \texttt{slky} (Offset
  \texttt{0x1922b}).
\item
  \textbf{Memory Extraction:} Use the following logic to dump the
  finalized key immediately before it is passed to the XOR loop:
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}

\NormalTok{Interceptor}\OperatorTok{.}\FunctionTok{attach}\NormalTok{(moduleBase}\OperatorTok{.}\FunctionTok{add}\NormalTok{(}\BaseNTok{0x1922b}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \DataTypeTok{onLeave}\OperatorTok{:} \KeywordTok{function}\NormalTok{(retval) \{}

        \BuiltInTok{console}\OperatorTok{.}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{hexdump}\NormalTok{(retval}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ \{ }\DataTypeTok{length}\OperatorTok{:} \DecValTok{32}\OperatorTok{,} \DataTypeTok{header}\OperatorTok{:} \KeywordTok{true}\NormalTok{ \}))}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\})}\OperatorTok{;}
