\subparagraph{2.4 Advanced Evasion: Hardware-Level
Sandboxing}\label{24-advanced-evasion-hardware-level-sandboxing}

To successfully execute the Frida-based "Ghost" Hook, the environment
must bypass multi-stage anti-analysis checks that specifically target
virtualized environments and debugging frameworks.

2.4.1 The "Real Machine" Requirement

Modern iApp payloads often query the hardware abstraction layer (HAL) to
detect emulators. To counter this, a \textbf{Real Machine Sandbox (RMS)}
is mandatory.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Hardware Spoofing:} Use a physical Android device with a
  "User-Debug" build or a rooted stock ROM.
\item
  \textbf{Thermal/Sensor Validation:} Malware often checks for the
  presence of a battery, accelerometer data, or light sensor changes. An
  RMS ensures these system properties return realistic, fluctuating
  values that an emulator cannot easily simulate.
\item
  \textbf{Network Fingerprinting:} Emulators often share the
  host\textquotesingle s bridge IP. The RMS should be placed behind a
  dedicated cellular hotspot or a VPN-configured router to hide
  data-center IP ranges.
\end{itemize}

2.4.2 Bypassing Heuristic "Tracer" Detection

The \texttt{slky} routine is protected by checks that monitor the
\texttt{/proc/self/status} and \texttt{/proc/self/maps} files for
strings like \texttt{frida}, \texttt{gum-js}, or a non-zero
\texttt{TracerPid}.

\textbf{Advanced Frida Stealth Techniques:}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Instruction Patching (The "Skip"):} Instead of hiding Frida,
  use Frida to patch the anti-debug function in memory. Find the code
  that calls \texttt{exit()} or \texttt{abort()} upon detecting a
  debugger and replace it with \texttt{NOP} (No-Operation) instructions
  or a \texttt{RET} (Return).
\item
  \textbf{Kernel-Level Masking (KPROBES):} For sophisticated samples,
  use a custom kernel (like \textbf{KernelSU}) that can hide the "su"
  binary and the "frida-server" process from the user-space entirely.
\item
  \textbf{D-Bus Protocol Obfuscation:} Standard Frida communication uses
  the D-Bus protocol on a fixed port. Compiling a custom version of
  Frida with a modified string for the D-Bus communication prevents
  "String Scanning" detection.
\end{enumerate}

2.4.3 Dynamic Context Recovery

The goal of the RMS is to reach the \textbf{"Decryption
Threshold"}---the point where the app believes it is safe and executes
\texttt{asendn}.

\textbf{The Strategy for the Analysis Team:}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Step 1:} Flash a clean, physical device with a "Hidden Root"
  solution (Magisk + DenyList or KernelSU).
\item
  \textbf{Step 2:} Deploy a custom-compiled Frida-Gadget as a
  \texttt{.so} library inside the APK itself, rather than using an
  external server. This makes the instrumentation look like a native
  part of the app\textquotesingle s own process.
\item
  \textbf{Step 3:} Use \textbf{Frida-Stalker} to follow the execution of
  the \texttt{slky} loop. This allows us to record every transformation
  made to the buffer without triggering "Breakpoint" detection.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{3. Structural Analysis: The iApp/Ant Studio
Framework}\label{3-structural-analysis-the-iappant-studio-framework}

The malware is built on the \textbf{iApp (Ant Studio)} development
framework, common in Chinese-origin grayware and Trojans.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Dynamic Loading:} The \texttt{logoActivity.smali} code
  confirms the app scans assets for a file named \texttt{yuv0.xml}. This
  file likely serves as a manifest for the "real" payload once the
  native layer decrypts it into memory.
\item
  \textbf{The "Yu Code" Interpreter:} We\textquotesingle ve identified
  \texttt{com.iapp.app.run} and classes like \texttt{Aid\_YuCodeX} as
  the execution engine. This engine runs scripts (likely \texttt{.iyu}
  or \texttt{.myu} files) that are hidden inside the encrypted
  \texttt{lib.so}. These scripts perform the actual malicious actions
  (SMS intercepting, C2 communication) while the Java layer remains
  "clean."
\item
  \textbf{Stateful Control:} The variable
  \texttt{Lc/b/a/a/w;-\textgreater{}o:I} acts as a global state flag.
  When set by the native library, it likely switches the engine into an
  "Encrypted Path," forcing the app to read its logic from a decrypted
  memory buffer rather than the standard filesystem.
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ base64}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ re}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ get\_xor\_key():}
    \CommentTok{\# Replicating the Java: Base64Utils.encode("三生石畔 彼岸花开".getBytes())}
\NormalTok{    s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ base64.b64encode(}\StringTok{"三生石畔 彼岸花开"}\NormalTok{.encode(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}utf{-}8\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)).decode(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}utf{-}8\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
    \CommentTok{\# replaceAll("\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}D+", "") {-}\textgreater{} keep only numbers}
\NormalTok{    digits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ re.sub(}\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{\textbackslash{}D}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, s)}
    \CommentTok{\# reverse and convert to int}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{(digits[::}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ decrypt\_l(encrypted\_str):}
\NormalTok{    key }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ get\_xor\_key()}

    \CommentTok{\# 1. Reverse XOR with the dynamic key}
\NormalTok{    chars }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{list}\NormalTok{(encrypted\_str)}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(chars)):}
\NormalTok{        chars[i] }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{chr}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{ord}\NormalTok{(chars[i]) }\OperatorTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{ key)}

    \CommentTok{\# 2. Base64 Decode}
\NormalTok{    b64\_decoded }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ base64.b64decode(}\StringTok{""}\NormalTok{.join(chars))}

    \CommentTok{\# 3. XOR with 255 (Bitwise NOT)}
\NormalTok{    final\_bytes }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{bytearray}\NormalTok{()}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ b }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ b64\_decoded:}
\NormalTok{        final\_bytes.append(b }\OperatorTok{\^{}} \DecValTok{255}\NormalTok{)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ final\_bytes.decode(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}utf{-}8\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, errors}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}ignore\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Testing with the QQ group or other strings}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Dynamic Key Found: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{get\_xor\_key()}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\NormalTok{        key }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ get\_xor\_key()}

\subsubsection{4. Technical Discovery: The "Ghost Server"
Backdoor}\label{4-technical-discovery-the-ghost-server-backdoor}

The malware utilizes an embedded web server to turn the infected device
into a remote-controlled terminal.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{BeanShell Remote Shell:} The discovery of
  \texttt{bsh/util/Httpd.smali} confirms the presence of a mini-web
  server. Unlike standard apps that "pull" data from a server, this app
  "listens" for incoming commands.
\item
  \textbf{Persistent Listening:} The
  \texttt{ServerSocket-\textgreater{}accept()} loop in
  \texttt{Httpd.smali} indicates that once the app is launched, it opens
  a port on the device. An attacker can "push" raw Java code or system
  commands directly to the phone via a simple HTTP POST request.
\item
  \textbf{Multi-Threaded Execution:} The
  \texttt{Ljava/lang/Thread;-\textgreater{}start()V} calls within the
  \texttt{Httpd} class allow the attacker to execute tasks (like file
  exfiltration or audio recording) in the background without affecting
  the user\textquotesingle s current activity.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{5. Decoded Indicators of Compromise
(IOCs)}\label{5-decoded-indicators-of-compromise-iocs}

Using the \texttt{btoo} (Byte-to-Object) decryption logic found in the
scripts, we have successfully unmasked the "plain text" values that the
malware uses to hide its operational hub.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Encoded Decimal & Decoded Value & Role in Malware \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{55\ 51\ 52\ 54\ 50\ 56\ 51\ 56\ 55} & \textbf{734628387} &
\textbf{The QQ Group ID.} This is the primary command and control (C2)
hub for the "Ant Studio" developers. \\
\texttt{-26\ -72\ -87\ ...} & \textbf{温馨提示} & "Warm Reminder" ---
Used as the title for fake system popups. \\
\texttt{-28\ -72\ -117\ ...} & \textbf{下载更新} & "Download Update" ---
The button text used to trick users into installing more malicious
stages. \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{6. Defensive Infrastructure: The "China-Only"
Gatekeeper}\label{6-defensive-infrastructure-the-china-only-gatekeeper}

Our attempts to reach the command infrastructure revealed a high degree
of operational security (OPSEC).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{IP Geofencing:} The \texttt{Connection\ reset\ by\ peer} and
  Cloudflare blocks suggest the attackers use "Regional Gatekeeping."
  The servers are configured to reject any traffic that does not
  originate from a specific range of Chinese mobile carrier IP
  addresses.
\item
  \textbf{Anti-Sandboxing:} The infrastructure is tuned to ignore
  \texttt{curl} or automated research tools, likely validating the
  \texttt{User-Agent} and device fingerprints before allowing the
  payload to download.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{C2 Infrastructure Intelligence
Report}\label{c2-infrastructure-intelligence-report-2}

\textbf{Report ID:} C2-2026-0120-YGS

\textbf{Author:} Apich Organization Security Team

\textbf{Classification:} High-Risk / Active Surveillance Cluster

\textbf{Primary Target Region:} Mainland China / Hong Kong

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{1. Architectural
Overview}\label{1-architectural-overview-2}

The infrastructure utilizes a \textbf{Tiered Proxy-to-Backend} model.
This design ensures that if the front-end domain (\texttt{docq.cn}) is
flagged, the central database and payload repository
(\texttt{107.151.212.80}) remain hidden and reusable under different
domain aliases.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Tier & Component & Identity & Technical Role \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Tier 1} & \textbf{The Mask} & \texttt{docq.cn} & Acts as the
initial contact point. Routes traffic through Alibaba Cloud (US) to
bypass regional domestic monitoring. \\
\textbf{Tier 2} & \textbf{The Controller} & \texttt{ht.bu84.com} &
"Heartbeat" (HT) server. Manages persistent connections from infected
devices using IIS 10.0. \\
\textbf{Tier 3} & \textbf{The Vault} & \texttt{107.151.212.80:3306} &
Exposed MySQL database. Stores exfiltrated LogCat data, contacts, and
device metadata. \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{2. Forensic Attribution (WHOIS
Analysis)}\label{2-forensic-attribution-whois-analysis-2}

The registration data reveals a strong link to the \textbf{Fujian/Wuhan
gray-market ecosystem}, a known hub for the development of "iApp"
modification tools and game injectors.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Corporate Entity:}
  \texttt{Wuhan\ Zhiyun\ Software\ Co.,\ Ltd.} (武汉织云软件有限公司).
  This entity is likely either a shell company registered for this
  purpose or a legitimate firm whose infrastructure was hijacked to
  serve as a high-reputation proxy.
\item
  \textbf{Operational Pattern:} Domain registrations for this cluster
  (including \texttt{bu84.com}) strictly follow \textbf{UTC+8 business
  hours}, with significant pauses during Chinese New Year, confirming
  the threat actors are likely based in East Asia.
\item
  \textbf{Persistence:} \texttt{bu84.com} was updated on \textbf{January
  14, 2026}, indicating that this specific campaign is currently in its
  active "harvesting" phase.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{3. Detected Vulnerabilities. in
C2}\label{3-detected-vulnerabilities-in-c2-2}

The attackers have made several critical "Amateur-Hour" errors that can
be exploited:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Direct Database Exposure:} Port \texttt{3306} (MySQL) is open
  to the public. This suggests the Android app\textquotesingle s
  Lua/Java scripts might be hardcoded to write directly to this DB, a
  very "noisy" behavior.
\item
  \textbf{Management Leaks:} The presence of \texttt{phpMyAdmin} on port
  \texttt{999} is a classic hallmark of the \textbf{iApp-PHP-MySQL}
  stack. It provides a visual interface for the attackers to view stolen
  data, but it also serves as a high-confidence signature for security
  researchers.
\end{itemize}

This vulnerability poses a significant risk of sophisticated
cyberattacks, which could result in the mass exfiltration and disclosure
of Personally Identifiable Information (PII).

Noticeably, the attacker uses \texttt{phpMyAdmin} v5.2.0-rc.1 and
\texttt{MySQL} v8.0.39 which both have many known critical security
vulnerabilities.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{4. Correlation with APK
Analysis}\label{4-correlation-with-apk-analysis-2}

The "modular" DEX files we found earlier are designed to feed this
specific infrastructure:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{classes2.dex} (LogCat Reader) ➔ Feeds the \textbf{MySQL
  Database}.
\item
  \texttt{Compression.dex} (Zip4j) ➔ Uploads batches to the \textbf{IIS
  Web Server}.
\item
  \texttt{libygsiyu.so} (iApp) ➔ Connects to the \textbf{phpMyAdmin/PHP}
  management backend.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{5. Defensive Infrastructure: The "China-Only"
Gatekeeper}\label{5-defensive-infrastructure-the-china-only-gatekeeper-2}

Our attempts to reach the command infrastructure revealed a high degree
of operational security (OPSEC).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{IP Geofencing:} The \texttt{Connection\ reset\ by\ peer} and
  Cloudflare blocks suggest the attackers use "Regional Gatekeeping."
  The servers are configured to reject any traffic that does not
  originate from a specific range of Chinese mobile carrier IP
  addresses.
\item
  \textbf{Anti-Sandboxed-Requests:} The infrastructure is tuned to
  ignore \texttt{curl} or automated research tools, likely validating
  the \texttt{User-Agent} and device fingerprints before allowing the
  payload to download.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Reporting \&
Coordination}}{Reporting \& Coordination}}\label{reporting--coordination-2}

The initial report was submitted to the \textbf{TSRC} on January 20,
2026, at 13:43 CST, with subsequent revisions finalized at 17:29 and
17:59 CST. Due to mailing system errors and latency, multiple copies of
the reports were resubmitted again to the \textbf{TSRC} on January 21,
2026, using various methods (including mailing and wechat official
accounts of \textbf{TSRC}, e.g.); however, no response has been received
yet till the publishing of these reports. Thus, the reports are formally
submitted to CNCERT on January 21, 2026, at 21:44 CST.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Conclusion of Internal
Operations}}{Conclusion of Internal Operations}}\label{conclusion-of-internal-operations-2}

With the release of this acknowledgement, the \textbf{Apich Organization
Security Team} has officially concluded its primary internal tasks
regarding sample \textbf{YGS-2026-02}. However, we remain committed to
supporting the ongoing efforts of the \textbf{TSRC} and \textbf{CNCERT}.

Furthermore, our team will continue to actively monitor and track all
future variants within the \textbf{YGS} malware family.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Special
Thanks}}{Special Thanks}}\label{special-thanks-2}

We would like to extend our sincere gratitude to all members and
external contributors who provided invaluable assistance throughout this
analysis.
